Not a security issue — a `branch_manager` holds `ASSIGN_LEAD` and `VIEW_BRANCH_DATA` and can legitimately work the lead — but it changes who carries operational load, and it makes the escalation target and the work target the same person.
- **Why it is not decidable from evidence:** canon 04 says *"Reassign the lead to another **eligible employee**"* and defines neither "eligible" nor whether a manager qualifies. Both readings are defensible: loosely, any staff member placed in that team; strictly, a front-line lead-handling role, with the manager as escalation target rather than next handler.
- **Decision required:** whether the pool is filtered by role (and to which roles), whether managers are a last resort when no employee is available, and whether the `u.id` tie-break should instead be deterministic on something operational (e.g. current open-lead count).
- **Deliberately NOT changed:** this is pre-existing behaviour and `202607056600` is about who is NOTIFIED, not who receives the work. Conflating the two would have shipped an unrequested policy change inside a defect fix.

### ATTR-3 — acquisition lineage could be rewritten by anyone who could see the lead (discovered + FIXED 2026-08-29, `202607056700`)
- **Category:** attribution integrity / authorization · **Severity:** High · **Status:** FIXED · **Owner:** engineering
- **The rule, quoted rather than inferred.** Owner business intent, ATTRIBUTION BUSINESS RULE: *"Lead ownership and acquisition attribution are different concepts. Reassignment must never rewrite the original acquisition lineage. Preserve: original source, GCLID, FBCLID, UTM lineage, attribution_click_id, first-touch attribution, acquisition campaign/source. Changing the employee responsible for a lead must not change where the customer originally came from."* ORVION's own code already stated the same rule inline: `app.capture_attribution_click` attaches a click to a lead only `where attribution_click_id is null`, under the comment *"First-touch anchor"*. Nothing was invented here — a rule the RPC honoured was moved onto the table, where every writer meets it.
- **The §8-J question, answered by measurement.** Neither reassignment path rewrites attribution: `app.reassign_lead` updates `assigned_user_id`, `owner_user_id`, `owner_branch_id`, `owner_department_id`; `app.process_lead_sla` updates `assigned_user_id` and `owner_user_id`. Both were read line by line, and `64_acquisition_lineage_test` now asserts it on BOTH paths instead of trusting the read.
- **What was NOT true — the stronger half of the owner's rule.** `authenticated` holds UPDATE on `public.leads` and `scope_isolation` permits updating any lead the caller can see, which (canon 28 gives `employee` VIEW_DEPARTMENT_QUEUE) is their whole department's pipeline. Nothing prevented `update public.leads set attribution_click_id = <another click>` or `set lead_source_code = 'referral'`. Re-pointing a lead at a different click moves a future Google Ads conversion — and the revenue credited with it — from one campaign to another. Reachable by any employee, and invisible to the RPC's first-touch check because direct DML never passes through the RPC. **This is FIN-4 / ATTR-1's shape recurring on the attribution class.**
- **The sibling, found by asking what sits beside it:** `public.offline_conversions` is the REVENUE end of the same chain the owner names (*"Ad -> Click identifier -> ... -> Revenue"*) and carries `attribution_click_id`, `lead_id` and `marketing_campaign_id` of its own. `authenticated` holds UPDATE; `202607056000` added a capability guard (MANAGE_MARKETING_CAMPAIGN) and nothing made the record append-only, so a ceo or owner could re-point an ALREADY RECORDED conversion. Covered in the same migration rather than filed, because filing it would have left the class half-closed for the second time in one package.
- **Remediation (applied):** `app.forbid_acquisition_lineage_rewrite()`, a BEFORE UPDATE trigger taking its lineage columns as TG_ARGV and applying ONE rule to all of them — first touch: a lineage column may be established once (NULL -> value) and never changed after. Attached to `leads` (`attribution_click_id`, `lead_source_code`, `source_payload`) and `offline_conversions` (`attribution_click_id`, `lead_id`, `marketing_campaign_id`). Comparison through `to_jsonb` for SPEC-159-A's reason. **No session-less exemption, deliberately:** `capture_attribution_click` performs only the NULL -> value transition the guard permits and is the sole post-insert writer of `leads.attribution_click_id` in the entire database, and nothing updates the other lineage columns at all — so an exemption would buy nothing and open the one door the rule exists to close.
- **Deliberately NOT covered, each for a stated reason:** `attribution_clicks` itself — `202607056100` already revoked INSERT and UPDATE from `authenticated`, so a trigger there would guard a door with no handle; `leads.branch_id`/`department_id` and `offline_conversions.booking_id`/`payment_id`/`conversion_value` — organisational placement and commercial outcome, not acquisition lineage; deleting a click out from under a lead — already impossible, the FK is ON DELETE RESTRICT.
- **Test requirement (met):** `64_acquisition_lineage_test.sql` (21) — first touch proven as EXISTING RPC behaviour before the trigger is credited with it; a positive control proving the handler CAN update the lead, so every refusal is about the column and not about reach; all four rewrite shapes refused; item J asserted on both reassignment paths with a positive control proving each reassignment actually happened; the click record's own gclid, campaign and lead link proven intact; **a negative control proving ownership still moves**, since a trigger that froze the row would have satisfied every denial and broken the product; and the sibling proven with an actor who HOLDS MANAGE_MARKETING_CAMPAIGN (at aal2, because `guard_write_capability` charges `app.authorize`) so the refusal is of the act and not of the role.
- **Caught by an existing guard, and worth recording:** the first version omitted `revoke execute ... from public` on the new trigger function and `10_grant_model_test` §5 failed. POL-1 / GRANT-1's class — PostgreSQL's default is a grant, not a no-op — caught by the guard written for exactly that.

### LEAD-4 — nothing consumes `reassignment_blocked` on a scheduled run (recorded 2026-08-29)
- **Category:** observability · **Severity:** Low · **Status:** OPEN · **Owner:** engineering
- **Evidence:** `app.process_lead_sla` now returns `action = 'reassignment_blocked'` when no eligible handler exists, so the outcome is visible to any caller and is asserted by `65_eligible_lead_handler_test`. But the production caller is `pg_cron`, which discards the result set. A branch with no eligible handler therefore holds an overdue lead indefinitely, and only the earlier SLA warning (which DID reach the employee and both managers) says anything.
- **Why it was not solved here:** the fix is a persisted operational signal, and every available shape — a new `notification_type`, a new `event_type`, or a backlog function — is either new vocabulary (which canon owns) or SCHED-1's unbuilt monitoring surface. `app.storage_action_backlog()` is the precedent for the third; it is a separate package, not a line inside a defect fix.
- **Distinct from the silence it replaced:** before `202607056800` the same situation produced NO row at all, and the lead was instead handed to whoever happened to be placed nearby.

### GUARD-1 — `check_database_parity.ps1` reported "primary proven" from a value I supplied (discovered + MITIGATED 2026-08-29)
- **Category:** guard integrity · **Severity:** Medium · **Status:** MITIGATED (structural limit remains, now stated in the output) · **Owner:** engineering
- **Evidence, from this session:** `apply_migration` records its OWN generated `version` (e.g. `20260828085402`) rather than the repository's `202607056800`, so after deploying, Primary's ledger fingerprint was `ca253f453ee5b692fd6ed9aa97ac7140` while the repository produced `0c48b1fd30c03d2dcf3137cfb4b171f3`. I ran `check_database_parity.ps1 -PrimaryFingerprint 0c48b1fd...` — the repository's own expected value — and it printed **"Primary matches the repository"**. It was comparing the repository against itself.
- **Why it is structural, not a bug:** the script reaches the local database through docker/psql and cannot reach Primary at all (Primary is behind the MCP connector; AGENTS.md §6 keeps external credentials out of the agent). Its header already says the fingerprint must come "from a caller that can reach it". The defect is that its VERDICT did not repeat the caveat, and the verdict is what gets read.
- **Caught by:** querying Primary's ledger independently, which is what actually proved parity. The mistake survived the guard and did not survive the evidence.
- **Mitigation (applied):** the success branch now prints "(caller-supplied: this proves Primary parity only if that value was READ FROM Primary)". Deploys must also normalise the ledger rows `apply_migration` writes, back to the repository's `version`/`name` convention — done for both migrations in this package, and Primary's own ledger now independently produces `0c48b1fd30c03d2dcf3137cfb4b171f3`.
- **This is §4's own shape turned on the verification layer:** a guard whose positive result can be manufactured by its input proves nothing, and it is the guard I have been quoting as evidence in every session report.

### LEAD-2 — RESOLVED 2026-08-29: the capability already exists and canon already granted it
- **Category:** business vocabulary · **Severity:** was Low · **Status:** RESOLVED — NOT A GAP · **Owner:** —
- **What the register asked:** whether `walk_in` should join the `lead_source` catalog, since the owner directive names the walk-in customer as a primary Egyptian-agency scenario and filing footfall as `manual_entry` loses attribution fidelity.
- **The answer was already in canon, one heading above the value list.** canon 25 § `lead_source`: **"Ownership: Tenant-Extendable System Catalog"**, and **"Tenant additions: Allowed with admin permission."** The ten values are labelled *Default values*, not the closed set. Canon deliberately delegated this taxonomy to the tenant.
- **And the implementation honours it exactly.** `catalog_values.catalog_tenant_insert` requires `tenant_id = app.current_tenant_id()` AND `app.has_permission('MANAGE_TENANT_SETTINGS')` (held by ceo and owner); `app.enforce_catalog_codes` accepts a value whose `tenant_id` is null OR equal to the writing row's tenant. So an agency with footfall adds its own `walk_in` today, scoped to itself, gated by the admin permission canon names.
- **Why ORVION should NOT ship `walk_in` as a system default:** doing so would decide a market's acquisition taxonomy on canon's behalf in the one catalog canon expressly handed to the tenant, and it would put a value in every tenant's list whether or not they have a shopfront.
- **Test requirement (met):** `64_acquisition_lineage_test.sql` — an employee is REFUSED, the owner is permitted, a lead is then recorded against the new value, and a second tenant cannot use it. A capability canon promises and nobody has ever exercised is a claim, not a capability.

### LEAD-3 — RESOLVED 2026-08-29 (`202607056800`): the question answered, and a defect underneath it
- **Category:** operational policy · **Severity:** was Low–Medium; the defect found underneath it was High · **Status:** RESOLVED + FIXED · **Owner:** engineering
- **The stated question — do managers belong in the reassignment pool? — is answered by the permission matrix, not by the word "employee".** Live: `CLOSE_LEAD`, `CREATE_LEAD`, `CREATE_QUOTATION` and `VIEW_DEPARTMENT_QUEUE` all resolve to the IDENTICAL six roles — branch_manager, ceo, department_manager, employee, owner, senior_employee. By ORVION's own definition of what it takes to work a lead, a manager IS an eligible handler; it also matches how a small Egyptian agency runs, where the branch manager sells alongside the team. **Current behaviour correct; managers stay.**
- **The defect underneath, REPRODUCED before it was fixed.** The pool was never "eligible employees" — it was *everyone PLACED in the branch and department*, with no reference to what any of them may do. One handler, one trainee, one finance manager, two SLA passes: pass 1 -> `warned`, pass 2 -> `reassigned`, and the lead was then held by **Trainee | role=trainee | can_close_lead=f | can_quote=f**. `trainee` holds exactly two permissions in the entire system — VIEW_ASSIGNED_LEADS and VIEW_ASSIGNED_TASKS. An SLA-overdue lead, the one case where ORVION intervenes *because revenue is at risk*, was handed to the one role that cannot quote it, close it or book it. `finance_manager` and `system_administrator` were equally eligible. **The rescue mechanism could park a lead where it can never leave.**
- **Root cause — SEC-1's shape, in the one place with no human in the loop:** a capability decision taken by PROXIMITY (who is placed here) instead of by AUTHORITY (who may do this).
- **Remediation (applied):** `app.eligible_lead_handlers(tenant, branch, department, exclude)` — active users placed in the lead's branch AND department who hold `CLOSE_LEAD` through an active role assignment, resolved the way `app.has_permission` resolves it (active assignment, active role, active permission) for an arbitrary user, since this runs with no session. CLOSE_LEAD carries no `required_feature_code`, so there is no plan gate to mirror. The choice of permission narrows nothing the other three candidates would not narrow identically — all four resolve to the same six roles — so it is a reading of the matrix rather than a preference between readings.
- **When nobody is eligible** the lead now STAYS with its current assignee and the pass reports `reassignment_blocked`, instead of moving to someone who cannot act. See **LEAD-4** for what still does not consume that signal.
- **Deliberately NOT changed:** the round-robin ordering (least-recently-assigned, then `u.id` — the primary key of the ordering is already operational; the tie-break only separates people who have never held a lead, and changing it is an optimisation); the 15/30 windows; the warning path and SLA-1's manager escalation; the event vocabulary; assignment-history immutability.
- **`app.reassign_lead`, the HUMAN path, is INTENTIONALLY untouched — and this is settled, not deferred.** ORVION deliberately supports a trainee holding an assigned lead: `app.require_lead_handler` admits the assigned handler regardless of permission, and `scripts/verify_lifecycle_branches.ps1` already asserts *"a trainee CAN log an interaction on the lead they are ASSIGNED — the handler rule, not a permission"* alongside the refusals that stop them creating their own. A supervisor naming a trainee is a deliberate act with existing, tested meaning. The automatic path is different **because no human chose**.
- **Test requirement (met):** `65_eligible_lead_handler_test.sql` (12) — a positive control proving all six are PLACED here, so every exclusion is about authority; the eligible set proven EXACTLY (`set_eq`), covering trainee, finance manager, a deactivated user and an EXPIRED role assignment; the manager's eligibility pinned as the answer to the original question; a cross-tenant negative control; one pass producing TWO outcomes (Cairo rescued, Alexandria blocked); and the blocked lead proven to have moved nothing — no assignee change, no notification, **no assignment-history row for a reassignment that did not occur**. The fixture is load-bearing: every excluded user sorts BEFORE the colleague who must win, so a failed exclusion names the wrong person rather than passing quietly.

### RBAC-2 — RESOLVED 2026-08-29: there is no tenant-side role administration to enforce
- **Category:** misleading contract · **Severity:** was Low · **Status:** RESOLVED — INTENTIONAL (architectural) · **Owner:** —
- **What the register asked:** whether role administration should begin charging `MANAGE_ROLES` (rather than `MANAGE_USERS`), or whether the two permissions are obsolete.
- **Live evidence that settles it:** `roles`, `role_permissions` and `permissions` grant `authenticated` **SELECT only**, and each carries exactly one RLS policy — `read_all_authenticated`, `cmd = SELECT`. Writes belong to `service_role`. **ORVION's role model is fixed and platform-owned:** a tenant cannot create a role, edit a role's permissions, or invent a permission. So `MANAGE_ROLES` and `MANAGE_PERMISSIONS` do not gate a capability that is enforced in the wrong place — they gate a capability ORVION deliberately does not offer to tenants.
- **Why granting them to `app.assign_user_role` would have been WRONG:** assigning a role to a user is *user* administration, which `MANAGE_USERS` correctly governs (RBAC-1). Charging `MANAGE_ROLES` there would have attached the role-administration permission to something that is not role administration, making the contract more misleading rather than less — and it would have changed nothing behaviourally, since both permissions are held by exactly {ceo, owner}, the same holders as MANAGE_USERS.
- **Already guarded:** the three tables are not `authenticated`-insertable, so `10_grant_model_test`'s SEC-1 ceiling (at most 54 insertable tables) already fails the day one of them becomes writable — which is precisely the day this classification must be revisited. No new assertion was added, because a redundant one implies the ceiling does not cover it.
- **Related:** PERM-1, the same question for three permissions granted to nobody.

### PERM-1 — RESOLVED 2026-08-29: all three are PLAN entitlements, and API-1 does not make them applicable
- **Category:** vocabulary · **Severity:** was Low · **Status:** RESOLVED — INTENTIONAL (premature, not dead) · **Owner:** —
- **The dependency the register named has landed** — API-1 closed on 2026-08-28 — so the question it deferred ("API-1 must decide whether the public endpoint surface is gated by them") is now answerable.
- **Live evidence:** all three carry a `required_feature_code` — `ACCESS_API_FULL -> api_full`, `ACCESS_API_READ_ONLY -> api_read_only`, `VIEW_ADVANCED_DASHBOARDS -> advanced_dashboards` — and `app.has_permission` already composes `app.plan_allows(required_feature_code)`. They are plan-tier entitlement gates with a working mechanism behind them, matching canon 28's Feature Access By Plan table (API Read Only: Starter No / Professional Yes / Enterprise Yes).
- **The answer, and it is a NO:** the endpoint surface API-1 exposed must NOT be gated by `ACCESS_API_*`. Those 71 wrappers and 8 reporting views are **ORVION's own application data path** — the WeWeb frontend calls exactly these PostgREST endpoints — so gating them on `api_full` would disable the product for every plan that lacks the feature. `ACCESS_API_*` describe a THIRD-PARTY integration API (tenant-held API keys or service accounts), which ORVION does not offer; there is no such surface to gate.
- **Held by no role, correctly:** granting a role a permission whose capability does not exist would make the permission matrix claim a capability. `VIEW_ADVANCED_DASHBOARDS` stays with **PLAN-1**, the three undefined "Limited" plan ceilings.
- **Trigger for revisiting:** the first tenant-facing API-key or service-account feature, or the first "advanced" dashboard. Either brings its own gating assertion.

### TASK-3 — RESOLVED 2026-08-29: canon did not omit a start event, it declined one
- **Category:** vocabulary / canon · **Severity:** was Low · **Status:** RESOLVED — INTENTIONAL · **Owner:** —
- **Evidence:** canon 26's Task State Machine describes `open -> in_progress` as *"Responsible employee starts work"* and `overdue -> in_progress` as *"Work resumed on an overdue task"* — it names both transitions explicitly — and its Required Events list is five long: `task_created`, `task_assigned`, `task_completed`, `task_cancelled`, `task_overdue`. Canon described the transition and then did not list an event for it. **That is a choice on the record, not a silence.**
- **Consequence, accepted:** the moment work begins is not queryable from the event spine. It remains in `tasks.task_status_code` and `tasks.updated_at`. TASK-2 already established the principle that a missing record is recoverable while a false one silently corrupts every consumer that trusts it — which is why `advance_task`'s two start transitions emit nothing.
- **Already guarded:** `54_transition_permission_parity_test` compares both sources including the `null::text` event slot, so a future engineer cannot quietly add an event to one source only.

### ORPH-1 — RESOLVED 2026-08-29: orphans are reviewed, never destroyed on sight
- **Category:** business / operations · **Severity:** was Low · **Status:** RESOLVED — INTENTIONAL · **Owner:** —
- **The three options the register listed** were: destroy after a quarantine period, export first, or review individually.
- **The evidence chooses.** A tenant **cannot create an orphan through the API** — the storage INSERT policy requires a matching `document_versions` row, proven over HTTP by *"upload with no metadata row is REFUSED"*. An orphan therefore implies a PLATFORM-SIDE failure: bytes whose metadata transaction did not commit. That is not garbage; it is a customer's document ORVION has lost the index to, and destroying it is irreversible and makes recovery impossible. Mature practice for an unexplained artefact in a system of record is to surface it for a human, not to reclaim the disk. DEL-1 independently means the database cannot destroy an object at all.
- **The decision is therefore the implemented behaviour, confirmed rather than changed:** `app.claim_storage_actions` hands out only `retention_expired`; `orphan_object` findings are surfaced, counted by `app.storage_action_backlog()`'s `unresolved_findings`, and acted on by a person. Already asserted by `60_storage_backlog_observability_test` and `scripts/verify_storage_end_to_end.ps1` ("a REAL orphan object is detected").
- **What would reopen this:** an operational history showing orphans arising routinely rather than exceptionally, which would make them a volume problem instead of an incident.

### FIN-5 — NARROWED 2026-08-29: six of the seven approval types have no producer
- **Category:** authorization / canon · **Severity:** Low · **Status:** **✅ RESOLVED 2026-09-01 (OWNER-1) — DECIDED BY THE OWNER, narrowed: the six unbuilt types bring their opening permission when built.** EVT-2's class, on a trigger — not a live question. **Superseded:** BLOCKED — BUSINESS DECISION (narrowed to the unbuilt types) · **Owner:** owner
- **Live evidence:** the `approval_type_code` catalog holds seven values — `finance_execution_approval`, `refund_approval`, `discount_approval`, `booking_override`, `manual_price_change`, `sensitive_data_change`, `subscription_approval`. A scan of every `app` function body for `insert into public.approval_requests` returns **exactly one function**: `app.request_finance_approval`.
- **The reachable half is already answered:** `202607056000` guards `approval_requests` INSERT with `CREATE_BOOKING_ITEM`, which is precisely the map FIN-2 established for the only type ORVION produces. FIN-4 makes the requester underivable-by-the-caller on every path.
- **What remains is EVT-2's class, not a permission decision:** six approval types are registered vocabulary with no producer. Deciding which permission opens a `discount_approval` requires first deciding that ORVION offers discount approval and what it does — a feature, not an authorization map. Recording the per-type permission before the workflow exists would freeze a guess into the security model.
- **Trigger:** whichever package builds the first of the six. It brings its opening permission and a positive/negative pair with it.

### VOID-1 — NARROWED 2026-08-29: canon DOES define a voided invoice state
- **Category:** missing capability · **Severity:** Medium · **Status:** BLOCKED — BUSINESS DECISION (narrowed) · **Owner:** owner
- **New evidence found while auditing the catalogs:** `invoice_status_code` holds **`voided`** as an active catalog value, alongside draft, issued, partially_paid, paid and overdue. So the earlier framing — "whether voiding is a capability ORVION offers at all" — is partly answered: canon registered the state. What is still absent is any transition into it (`app.status_transitions` has no `invoices` rows at all), any writer of `voided_at`/`voided_by`/`void_reason`, and any permission.
- **Still genuinely undecided, and each affects the schema:** which permission voiding costs (canon 28 defines none); which states it is legal from; and whether an invoice raised in error is instead corrected by a CREDIT NOTE — the more usual accounting answer, which would make `voided` a state that only ever applies to a never-issued draft, and the three columns largely wrong. `journal_entries` carries the same three columns with no catalog state behind them at all, and reversal rather than voiding is the standard for a posted ledger entry.
- **Why it is not resolvable from the accumulated intent:** the owner's business intent covers Refund/Cancellation as a booking-side journey and says nothing about correcting an issued invoice. Choosing void-vs-credit-note is choosing an accounting model.

### CONV-1 — a lapsed tenant's conversions were DESTROYED, not deferred (discovered + FIXED 2026-08-29, `202607056900`)
- **Category:** data loss / attribution integrity · **Severity:** High · **Status:** FIXED · **Owner:** engineering
- **Evidence (reproduced before fixing, then re-run against the fix).** `app.map_outcomes_to_conversions` filtered restricted tenants OUT of its set-based INSERT and then advanced `integration_cursors.last_seq` past their events unconditionally. Two tenants, one attributed lead each, both with a `lead_qualified` event, tenant B in `read_only`:
  ```
  run 1 (B read_only)      inserted 1   conv-good 1 | conv-lapsed 0
  B restored to good standing
  run 2                    inserted 0   conv-good 1 | conv-lapsed 0
  run 3                    inserted 0   conv-good 1 | conv-lapsed 0
  ```
  After the fix, the identical probe: run 2 inserts **1**, conv-lapsed **1**, run 3 inserts 0.
- **Why it is a defect and not a business decision — ORVION had already decided what "skip" means.** WP-03 established that batch callers skip lapsed tenants; everywhere else that means DEFER. `process_lead_sla` skips and retries sixty seconds later; `platform_resolve_storage_finding` refuses explicitly and the finding stays open (RET-2). The mapper was the only place skip meant DISCARD, and only because it owns a cursor. It also contradicts the owner's GOOGLE ADS requirement (*"Preserve attribution lineage from acquisition through revenue"*) and canon 28's promise that a restricted tenant's data stays intact — and it harms precisely the tenant with a commercial reason to care, the one who paid late.
- **Two remedies rejected, recorded so they are not re-proposed:** (1) *do not advance the cursor past a skipped event* — correct on loss, but converts one tenant's lapse into head-of-line blocking for every tenant, and a departed tenant stalls the mapper permanently; (2) *re-scan history for conversion events with no `offline_conversions` row* — needs no storage and reuses the `source_event_seq` key, but the permanently-unconvertible remainder (events whose lead carries no attribution click) grows without bound and starves the backfill's own limit.
- **Remediation (applied):** every event the gate filters out is recorded as an `item_deferred` finding BEFORE the cursor moves past it, and each run reconsiders the deferrals whose tenant can be written again. The cursor still advances, so no tenant blocks another. Recovery is safe to repeat because `offline_conversions.source_event_seq` is unique with `on conflict do nothing` — the idempotency key this fix leans on already existed. Deferrals self-close two ways: mapped, or reconsidered-while-writable and found to map to no attributed lead.

### SCHED-2 — two of three cron jobs could be killed by a single row, and said nothing (discovered + FIXED 2026-08-29, `202607056900`)
- **Category:** operational resilience · **Severity:** High (latent) · **Status:** FIXED · **Owner:** engineering
- **The audit (directive §7, every background path traced rather than skimmed).** Six background paths exist: three pg_cron jobs (`process_lead_sla` every minute, `process_subscription_lifecycle` daily 00:10, `reconcile_document_storage` daily 00:30), the n8n-driven `map_outcomes_to_conversions`, and the two lease loops (storage executor, conversion delivery). Compared side by side:

  | job | one item aborts all? | failure persisted? | discoverable after? |
  |---|---|---|---|
  | `reconcile_document_storage` | NO (per-tenant exception block) | YES (`tenant_scan_failed`) | YES |
  | `process_lead_sla` | **YES** | **NO** | **NO** |
  | `process_subscription_lifecycle` | **YES** | **NO** | **NO** |
  | `map_outcomes_to_conversions` | **YES** (set-based) | **NO** | **NO** |

- **The exemplar was already in the codebase.** `reconcile_document_storage` wraps each tenant in `begin ... exception when others then ... end`, records a finding, resolves it when a later scan of the same tenant succeeds, counts failures into its return value, and nests a second handler in case recording the failure itself fails. Its three siblings had none of that. This is not a new pattern; it is the in-house one, applied where it was missing.
- **Latency, stated honestly:** no naturally reachable raise exists in either loop body today — every write was traced. The defect is LATENT. It is fixed anyway because the property under test is isolation, and a fix aimed at today's reachable raise sources would measure the list rather than the property; the list is what changes. WP-03 was exactly that: a trigger correct for a user write and dangerous inside a batch.
- **What a stalled job actually costs:** `process_subscription_lifecycle` drives the state that gates every write in ORVION, so a single raising tenant would silently keep lapsed tenants writable and paying tenants un-renewed, every day, until someone read `cron.job_run_details` — which is in the `cron` schema, unexposed to PostgREST and ungranted to `service_role`, so no operator can reach it through the API at all.
- **Remediation (applied):** per-item `begin ... exception` isolation in `process_lead_sla` (per lead) and `process_subscription_lifecycle` (per tenant); a whole-body handler in `map_outcomes_to_conversions` that leaves the cursor un-advanced so the batch retries. Failures land in `public.scheduled_job_findings` and self-heal when a later run processes the same item.
- **The store, and why it is not new architecture:** `public.scheduled_job_findings` mirrors `public.document_storage_findings` — upsert on a natural key, `last_seen_at`, `attempt_count`, `resolved_at`, re-observation reopens. That table appears NOWHERE in canon: it is engineering-owned platform-operational state, created by WP-04 without a canon amendment, because operational health is not business vocabulary. `finding_type_code` is a CHECK rather than a catalog family (its model's codes are a domain vocabulary returned to an executor over HTTP; these two are an internal discriminator that is never rendered). Deliberately NOT built: no notification, no event type, no alert routing. §8 asks for enough evidence to answer nine questions; a table and a reader answer all nine.
- **Deliberately exempt from the subscription write gate**, and the reason is the inverse of the usual one: a restricted tenant is precisely the case those rows exist to RECORD. Gating the table would make a deferral unrecordable for exactly the tenant being deferred — which is how CONV-1's loss happened. Declared in `35_subscription_write_gate_test`'s exemption list with that reasoning attached.
- **Test requirement (met):** `66_scheduled_job_isolation_test.sql` (20) — CONV-1's full defer-and-recover cycle with a positive control that the mapper works at all, the cursor proven to still advance (so no head-of-line blocking), and a third run proving recovery is idempotent; then, for each isolated job, an INJECTED raise on one fixture row with `lives_ok` on the pass, **a positive control proving the healthy item was actually processed** (so the pass survived by working, not by doing nothing), the poisoned item proven untouched rather than half-applied, the failure's `sqlstate` proven to outlive the invocation, and automatic recovery once the raise source is removed. Plus the reader's boundary: refused to an authenticated tenant user at the GRANT layer, not merely emptied by RLS. Plus 3 HTTP assertions in `verify_storage_end_to_end.ps1`, because pgTAP cannot prove reachability — API-1 was 600 green assertions over an unreachable API.
- **Siblings audited and found CLEAN (§26), recorded so the sweep is verifiable:** the conversion-delivery lease already persists failure (`delivery_status_code = 'failed'`, `error_message`, `failed_at`, plus an `offline_conversion_failed` event) and recovers through SPEC-123's 30-minute lease; the storage executor already does the same through FND-1. Neither needed changing. What both still lack is an OPERATOR SURFACE for work that has exhausted its retries — see **DELIV-1**.

### LEAD-4 — RESOLVED 2026-08-29 (`202607056900`)
- **Category:** observability · **Severity:** was Low · **Status:** FIXED · **Owner:** engineering
- Recorded the same day it was created. `app.process_lead_sla` returned `reassignment_blocked` and pg_cron discarded it. The condition is now also written to `public.scheduled_job_findings` as `item_deferred` with the reason (*"no active user in this branch and department holds CLOSE_LEAD"*), so it survives the invocation and is visible through `public.scheduled_job_health()`. Asserted by `66_scheduled_job_isolation_test`.

### PAR-1 — the parity guard had never compared the function surface (discovered + FIXED 2026-08-29)
- **Category:** guard integrity / deployment hygiene · **Severity:** High (it is the guard every session report cites) · **Status:** FIXED · **Owner:** engineering
- **Evidence:** a full-surface comparison of all 228 `app` + `public` functions found **six** whose stored source on Primary differed from local: `add_customer_contact_method`, `assign_task`, `claim_conversion_deliveries`, `create_marketing_campaign`, `create_supplier`, `record_offline_conversion`. Local is rebuilt from `supabase/migrations` by `db reset`, so local IS the repository by construction; Primary had drifted.
- **Behaviour was NOT affected, and that was established before anything was changed:** under whitespace-insensitive comparison all 228 functions hashed identically on both environments (`19416a6b008f7202fe61fccac6e814e4`). The six differences were formatting and STRIPPED COMMENTS — hand-transcribed, reflowed SQL applied to Primary by earlier sessions through `apply_migration`, because `supabase db push` requires a database password and external credentials never pass through the agent (AGENTS.md §6).
- **Why it still matters:** the deployed system's self-documentation was worse than the repository's — `create_supplier` on Primary had lost the paragraph explaining why supplier names are compared case-insensitively — and, far more importantly, **nothing would have caught a real divergence.** Parity had only ever compared (a) the migration-ledger fingerprint, which proves the same migration NAMES were applied and says nothing about what the applied SQL created, and (b) the logic hash of the functions each package had just changed. Drift anywhere else was invisible by construction. This is §3's instruction turned on the verification layer: *"If an existing guard reports something as CLEAN, independently verify what the guard actually measures."*
- **Remediation (applied), in three parts:** (1) the six functions re-applied to Primary with the repository's exact text, after which the strict byte-level hash matches on both sides (`4821a18a9bf8193a4bc8c7dea6e345a8`, read independently from each); (2) `check_database_parity.ps1` gains `-PrimaryLogicHash`, a new Check L2/P2 covering the FULL function surface, and a verdict line that names the ledger and the functions separately rather than collapsing both into the word "proven"; (3) the ledger rows those repairs created were deleted, because nothing in the repository changed — the repair made Primary match what the existing migrations already specify, so inventing a migration file for it would have been a no-op replay.
- **Permanent deployment rule, recorded because it is the root cause:** a migration deployed through `apply_migration` must be pasted VERBATIM from its repository file. This package's own nine new functions were, and they matched byte-for-byte on the first comparison.

### DELIV-1 — delivery work that exhausts its retries has no operator surface (recorded 2026-08-29)
- **Category:** observability · **Severity:** Low · **Status:** OPEN (subsumed by PH8-2's decision) · **Owner:** owner then engineering
- **Evidence:** audited as SCHED-2's sibling and found already correct on the properties that matter — a failed delivery is durably `failed` with its `error_message`, and SPEC-123's lease recovers a crashed worker. What does not exist is any surface that shows an operator a conversion which has burned all five attempts. There is no `reporting` view for it (the eight that exist are all tenant-facing sales/finance views), and `scheduled_job_health()` deliberately reads only its own table rather than growing a second source.
- **Why it was not built here:** PH8-1's own residual and PH8-2 already own this question, and PH8-2 is an owner decision about what a delivery-health surface should report (it must also distinguish "never eligible because consent was absent" from "failed"). Building half of it now would pre-empt that decision.

### SEC-1b — the ceiling counted UPDATE-only triggers, and twelve tables were open (discovered + FIXED 2026-08-29, `202607057000`)
- **Category:** authorization architecture / guard integrity · **Severity:** High · **Status:** FIXED · **Owner:** engineering
- **The guard, and what it actually measured.** `10_grant_model_test` pinned "at most 17 tables have NO capability trigger **on the direct write path**". Its detector asked only whether some non-internal trigger's function body mentions `app.authorize` / `app.has_permission`. It never asked WHEN the trigger fires. `app.enforce_status_transition` and `app.enforce_archive_authority` both call `app.authorize` and both are **BEFORE UPDATE ONLY** — so every status-bearing and every archivable table was credited with protection on a path it did not have.
- **Measured live:** insertable by `authenticated` **54**; no capability trigger as the detector counts **17**; no capability trigger THAT FIRES ON INSERT **30**. Credited but UPDATE-only: `bookings`, `complaints`, `conversations`, `customer_notes`, `customers`, `documents`, `leads`, `marketing_campaigns`, `passengers`, `quotations`, `service_requests`, `suppliers`, `tasks`. Re-running the RESIDUE predicate with the same correction turns **3 into 15** — three canon-34 auth artifacts (INTENTIONAL) and **twelve ordinary business tables with no capability check on their INSERT path at all**.
- **Reproduced, both halves one second apart, same actor:**
  ```
  trainee holds CREATE_COMPLAINT? f   SEND_MESSAGE? f   CREATE_CUSTOMER? f   CREATE_TASK? f
  app.create_complaint(...)                -> REFUSED: permission denied: CREATE_COMPLAINT
  insert into public.complaints (...)      -> 1 row
  insert into public.conversations (...)   -> 1 row
  ```
  The trainee satisfies the RLS policy through its `current_user_id() = owner_user_id` disjunct: naming YOURSELF as owner is enough. Also proven over the wire — `POST /rest/v1/complaints` with the trainee's JWT, which is the door a browser would actually find.
- **Why SEC-1 was recorded as closed with this open:** the closure rested on the ceiling, and the ceiling was measuring a different thing than its own description claimed. This is the fourth instance of *"a guard written against the first instance takes that instance's shape"*, and the second in two days where the guard itself was the defect (see **GUARD-1**, **PAR-1**).
- **Remediation (applied):** `app.guard_write_capability`'s CASE gains all twelve, each charging the permission **read out of that table's own creating RPC** — nothing chosen (`create_supplier` charges ASSIGN_SUPPLIER, so `suppliers` charges ASSIGN_SUPPLIER). Triggers attached **BEFORE INSERT ONLY**: charging CREATE_BOOKING on every UPDATE would break `advance_booking`, because `finance_manager` holds ISSUE_BOOKING and not CREATE_BOOKING — the same exception `202607056000` had to make for `approval_requests`, found the same way, by checking before writing.
- **The detector is corrected, not just the code:** both predicates in `10_grant_model_test` now require `(t.tgtype & 4) <> 0`. The middle ceiling therefore ROSE from 17 to 18 while the exposure FELL — both are the same correction, and the file says so, because a number moving the wrong way looks like a regression to the next reader.
- **Test requirement (met):** `67_care_capability_and_message_integrity_test.sql` §1-9 — the trainee refused at BOTH doors, the employee permitted at BOTH doors on the same table (so the refusals distinguish "the guard works" from "nothing works"), the corrected residue asserted at 3, all twelve proven guarded ON INSERT, and **none of them on UPDATE**. Plus `scripts/verify_care_journeys.ps1` over HTTP.

### SEC-2 — RESOLVED 2026-08-29: it was never one question, and only one half was a decision
- **Category:** authorization architecture · **Severity:** was Medium · **Status:** RESOLVED (INTENTIONAL half) + FIXED (defect half, see FIN-6) · **Owner:** —
- **The question as filed:** does editing an existing record cost (a) the create permission, (b) a new `EDIT_*` per family, or (c) nothing, because RLS scope is deliberately the whole control?
- **Reading the evidence split it in two, and only one half was ever a business decision.**
- **HALF ONE — DESCRIPTIVE fields: (c), INTENTIONAL, and DERIVED rather than assumed.** ORVION governs mutation by CONSEQUENCE, not by table. Three independent pieces of evidence say so: (1) the permission catalog contains only SPECIFIC mutation permissions — `EDIT_LOCKED_COST` (one field, after lock) and `UPDATE_BOOKING_ITEM_STATUS` (one status) — and no generic `EDIT_<entity>` anywhere; (2) a scan of every `app` function that issues `update public.<table>` shows they update STATUS, OWNERSHIP, MONEY, ATTRIBUTION, ARCHIVE FLAGS and IDENTITY MERGES — **not one updates a descriptive field**; (3) canon 28 names no such permission. There is no `update_customer`, `edit_booking` or `amend_complaint` among the 71 exposed endpoints, so the PostgREST table endpoint is not a bypass of an intended door — **it is the only door**, and RLS scope is the control ORVION chose for it.
- **Reproduced and bounded:** a `trainee` (`CREATE_LEAD = f`, `CLOSE_LEAD = f`) renamed the lead assigned to them (SUCCEEDED) and could not see or edit a colleague-owned complaint (0 rows, `UPDATE 0`). RLS scope holds; the exposure is descriptive columns of rows already readable. Pinned by assertion 7 of `68_financial_status_capability_test`, which proves a NON-governed column on the very same row still updates — so the boundary is recorded rather than merely argued.
- **HALF TWO — CONSEQUENCE-BEARING fields with no guard: DEFECTS, not decisions.** Searching for them found one that was serious and reachable: **FIN-6**, below. That is what the "business decision" was hiding.
- **What would reopen this:** a new field with business consequence added to a table whose guard does not name it. `68_...` assertion 11 checks every column named in the financial guard's map against the catalog, which is the class check for exactly that.

### ATTR-4 — a conversation message could name any colleague as its sender (discovered + FIXED 2026-08-29, `202607057000`)
- **Category:** audit integrity · **Severity:** Medium–High · **Status:** FIXED · **Owner:** engineering
- **Evidence (reproduced):** `app.send_conversation_message` sets `sender_user_id` from the session; direct DML did not have to. An employee inserted a message into their own conversation naming a COLLEAGUE, and the row read back `Colleague | I never wrote this`.
- **Why it matters more than the usual attribution defect:** `conversation_messages` is the record of what the agency SAID to a customer, on the channel canon 10 designates for customer communication and the one the future WhatsApp/AI layer will write to. A forged sender is a colleague on record having promised something they never said.
- **Remediation (applied):** `app.derive_message_sender()` — ATTR-1's shape. On INSERT with a session, `sender_user_id := app.current_user_id()`; on UPDATE, immutable. The rule is exactly what the RPC already does (it sets the actor whatever the `sender_type_code`), so the guard makes the RPC's behaviour unbypassable rather than introducing a second rule. Session-less writers keep what they set, so a future inbound-message integration is unaffected.
- **Test requirement (met):** asserted ON THE ROW rather than on the absence of an exception — "no exception raised" would have passed even if the forgery had stuck. Proven again over HTTP by POSTing to `/rest/v1/conversation_messages`.

### CONV-2 — a sent message could be rewritten or deleted (discovered + FIXED 2026-08-29, `202607057000`)
- **Category:** audit integrity · **Severity:** Medium · **Status:** FIXED · **Owner:** engineering
- **Evidence (reproduced):** `update public.conversation_messages set message_text = '...'` on a message the employee had sent returned `UPDATE 1` and the text was replaced. DELETE was open on the same `for ALL` policy.
- **Remediation (applied):** `app.forbid_message_rewrite()` freezes `conversation_id`, `tenant_id`, `sender_type_code`, `sender_user_id`, `message_direction_code`, `message_text`, `sent_at`, `received_at`, and forbids DELETE outright — the treatment ORVION already gives `events` (`app.forbid_mutation`) and lead assignment history (`app.forbid_assignment_history_rewrite`).
- **What is deliberately NOT frozen, and why the column list matters:** `external_message_id` and `metadata` remain writable. They are the DELIVERY integration's fields — a WhatsApp writer learns the provider's message id only after the send. A blanket row freeze would have looked stricter and broken the integration ORVION is being built for; the same reasoning `forbid_assignment_history_rewrite` uses when it permits exactly `unassigned_at` and `is_current`. Asserted from both sides.

### COMP-1 — a complaint could reach `resolved` with no record of how (discovered + FIXED 2026-08-29, `202607057000`)
- **Category:** misleading contract / operational completeness · **Severity:** Low–Medium · **Status:** FIXED · **Owner:** engineering
- **Evidence:** `public.complaints.resolution_notes` is declared in canon 31 ("resolution_notes nullable") and a repository-wide search finds it written by **nothing** — VOID-1's class. Meanwhile `app.advance_complaint` already accepts `p_reason` and already puts it in the event.
- **Remediation (applied), deliberately narrow:** on the transition INTO `resolved`, and only that one, the reason is persisted as the resolution note. `resolved` is the single transition where "reason" and "resolution" denote the same fact; writing `p_reason` on `new -> acknowledged` would corrupt the column rather than fill it. `coalesce` so an empty call does not erase an earlier note. No signature change, no new parameter, no new vocabulary.
- **Why this is a fix and VOID-1 is not:** voiding needs a permission canon does not define, a set of legal source states, and a void-versus-credit-note decision. This needed none of those — the RPC, the authority, the parameter and the column all already existed and simply were not joined.
- **Test requirement (met):** the note asserted after a real resolve, plus a NEGATIVE control on a complaint that was never resolved (its note stays null), which is what pins the write to that one transition.

### TEST-2 — fixture collision on `auth.users.email`, which no scoping discipline covered (discovered + FIXED 2026-08-29)
- **Category:** test integrity · **Severity:** Medium · **Status:** FIXED · **Owner:** engineering
- **Evidence:** Pass A was green at 67 files / 805 assertions; **Pass B died** — `67_care_capability_and_message_integrity_test.sql` reported `Bad plan. You planned 24 tests but ran 0`, on `duplicate key value violates unique constraint "users_email_partial_key" ... Key (email)=(emp@care.test) already exists`. The new HTTP suite and the new pgTAP file, written in the same session, had both used `emp@care.test`.
- **Root cause — a gap in an existing discipline, not a new class.** The slug-collision rule established after `lc-branch-travel` covered `tenants.slug` and `branches.slug`. The colliding key here is `auth.users.email`, whose unique index is **`CREATE UNIQUE INDEX users_email_partial_key ON auth.users (email) WHERE (is_sso_user = false)`** — Supabase's own, global across the entire database, with no tenant column to scope by. It is the one identifier every fixture in both suites writes and no rule mentioned.
- **Remediation (applied):** the pgTAP fixture moved to `@care-pgtap.test`. Pass B then ran 805 again.
- **Class swept, not just the instance:** a scan of every `insert into auth.users` block across all 67 test files and all 10 scripts found **117 distinct fixture emails, 25 owned by a persistent HTTP suite, and ZERO collisions** involving a persistent suite — so this was the only occurrence. Recorded because pgTAP files roll back and therefore may safely share an address; only a PERSISTENT suite's addresses are dangerous, which is the distinction the rule needs to state.

### PAR-1a — the parity comparison I wrote to fix PAR-1 was itself measuring a subset (discovered + FIXED 2026-08-29)
- **Category:** guard integrity · **Severity:** Medium · **Status:** FIXED · **Owner:** engineering
- **A correction to the 2026-08-28 report, stated plainly:** it reported all 228 functions byte-identical across local and Primary at `4821a18a9bf8193a4bc8c7dea6e345a8`. That comparison used `regexp_replace(def, '--[^\n]*', '', 'g')`. **Inside a POSIX bracket expression the backslash is not an escape**, so `[^\n]` reads as "not a backslash and not the letter n" — the pattern stops at the first `n` in a comment and leaves most comment text in the hash. The claim of byte-identity held only under that weaker normalization.
- **What it was hiding:** `check_database_parity.ps1`'s own Check L2/P2, which builds the pattern correctly with `chr(10)`, immediately reported PRIMARY FUNCTION DRIFT. Bucket-narrowing found exactly one function: **`app.document_retention_days`** — local `select null::integer`, Primary `\n    select null::integer;\n`.
- **Behaviour was verified before anything was touched:** both return NULL, and `select app.document_retention_days()` on Primary returns null, so **RET-1's retain-forever default is intact and no document is eligible for destruction on either environment.** It is PAR-1's cosmetic class in one more function — but it was a real difference that a session had reported as absent.
- **Remediation (applied):** Primary's source restored to the repository's exact text (ledger row removed afterwards, since nothing in the repository changed); the guard's header now states why `'--[^\n]*'` must never be used for this comparison; and the canonical value going forward is the guard's, not an ad-hoc query's. Full-surface parity now proven at **`1c63f2545d2452cece517e324c5b25c7`** across 230 functions, read independently from both.

### API-2 — the capability contract exists, and it is generated rather than written (delivered 2026-08-29)
- **Category:** interface governance · **Severity:** n/a (deliverable) · **Status:** DELIVERED · **Owner:** engineering
- **What was built:** `scripts/generate-api-contract.ps1` derives `reports/master/MASTER_API_CONTRACT.md` from `pg_catalog` and `app.status_transitions`; `check_database_parity.ps1` gains **Check L3**, which regenerates the file and FAILS if the committed copy differs. Proven in both directions — tampering with the document made the guard report `CONTRACT STALE`, and regenerating cleared it.
- **Why generated:** a hand-maintained interface document is a claim about the system, and this programme has now spent three sessions on what unverified claims cost (GUARD-1, PAR-1/PAR-1a, SEC-1b). The same shape as `ai-map.json` and Check 7.
- **What it covers that an RPC-only contract would have missed:** PostgREST serves TABLES as well as functions, so `POST /rest/v1/complaints` is as reachable from a browser as `POST /rest/v1/rpc/create_complaint` — which is exactly where SEC-1b was found. §4 documents all **71** tenant-reachable tables with their SIUD grants, INSERT guard, UPDATE guard and RLS policies.
- **A defect found in the generator itself, before it shipped:** the first version chose the permission by first-match CASE, so `advance_lead` reported only `CLOSE_LEAD` and silently omitted the TRANS-2 handler rule that governs its other transitions. Composed now (`CLOSE_LEAD + per transition: ASSIGN_LEAD, CLOSE_LEAD`). Recorded because a contract that understates authority is worse than no contract, and it is the same first-instance-shape failure as SEC-1b.
- **Also corrected before shipping:** the HTTP-coverage column initially reported **1** endpoint covered, because the suites call through a `Rpc $VAR 'name'` helper and the literal `rpc/<name>` never appears in their text. Detection now matches the two real call shapes exactly. A coverage column that over- or under-counts is the failure this contract exists to prevent.

### API-3 — exposed endpoints with no HTTP evidence (opened 2026-08-29 at 33 of 71)
- **Category:** test coverage · **Severity:** Medium · **Status:** ✅ CLOSED 2026-08-30 · **Owner:** engineering
- **CLOSURE (status field corrected 2026-09-02 — REG-3).** The debt was paid down family by family through 2026-08-30 (`MASTER_EXECUTION_PLAN.md` narrates each instalment and records "API-3 CLOSED — TASK-1 / TASK-2 / SUP-1"), and the GENERATED `MASTER_API_CONTRACT.md` §2 — derived from `pg_catalog` and diffed by `check_database_parity.ps1` Check L3 — now publishes **every executable endpoint as exercised by a suite, zero uncovered**. Read the count there, never here (GOV-5). **This field said `OPEN` for three days after that**, while `manifest.md`'s `Next capability` (copied into `ai-map.json` and read at every cold start) and the execution plan both said CLOSED — the findings SSOT contradicting three documents that were right. No guard could see it: Check 2 reads OPEN only from a padded table cell, and API-3 is defined by this detail block and has no table row. The paragraph below is retained as the **historical** reason it stayed open through 2026-08-29, not as current status.
- **The current count is NOT restated here, deliberately (GOV-5, 2026-08-30).** This block carried "30 of 71 … 41 of 71 are exercised" for two days while the real figures moved to 55 covered / 16 uncovered across four packages — a hand-maintained number in the SSOT drifting *below* the generated artifact it summarises. **Read the count from `MASTER_API_CONTRACT.md` §2, which is generated from the suites themselves and diffed by `check_database_parity.ps1` Check L3.** A number that must be retyped on every package is a stale number waiting to happen; the fix is to delete the restatement, not to refresh it.
- **Evidence:** `MASTER_API_CONTRACT.md` §2 marks each endpoint `yes`/`--` by whether a `verify_*.ps1` suite actually calls it. That column is a **REPOSITORY** fact — it measures suite source text, not an observed HTTP response (**VER-1**) — so it proves an endpoint is *reached by a suite*, and the suite's own run output is what proves the call succeeded.
- **Why it was not closed in the package that opened it (historical, 2026-08-29):** 33 endpoints × the six controls the owner specifies (positive, negative-permission, cross-tenant, validation, role difference, response shape) is ~200 assertions. Writing them in one sitting would be assertion-count theatre — which the owner's own directive names as the thing to avoid. The value is that the gap is now VISIBLE and payable down deliberately, endpoint by endpoint, as each is needed by the client.
- **Notable uncovered endpoints:** `create_journal_entry`, `create_department`, `create_marketing_campaign`, `merge_customer_identity`. API-1's standing lesson applies to every one: pgTAP proves database behaviour, and only HTTP proves the browser-facing door.

### CONV-3 — there is no session-less inbound door for a conversation (recorded 2026-08-29)
- **Category:** integration readiness · **Severity:** n/a today · **Status:** DEFER — belongs to the integration phase · **Owner:** engineering
- **The question asked:** can today's structures support `Customer → WhatsApp → AI agent → qualification → human employee → lead` WITHOUT architectural rework?
- **The data model already can, and this was verified rather than assumed:** `conversations.external_conversation_id` exists (the WhatsApp thread id); `conversations.customer_id` and `owner_user_id` are **nullable**, so a thread can exist before the customer is identified and before any human is assigned; `conversation_messages.sender_user_id` is nullable and `external_message_id` is present; `sender_type_code` already offers `customer`, `system` and `external_provider`; `channel_code` and `lead_source_code` both include `whatsapp`; and `capture_attribution_click` is already granted to `orvion_integration`, so a WhatsApp-originated click can be captured session-lessly.
- **What is missing is a DOOR, not a model.** All three conversation RPCs — `start_conversation`, `send_conversation_message`, `advance_conversation` — are SECURITY INVOKER and granted to `authenticated` ONLY; none is granted to `orvion_integration`. `start_conversation` additionally requires a branch placement (*"you have no primary branch assignment"*), which an inbound message from an unknown customer does not have. Today such a flow could only work by impersonating an employee JWT, which is precisely what must not be built.
- **Why this is DEFER and not a defect:** the integration phase is deliberately not started, and the precedent for the fix already exists in-house — `capture_attribution_click` and `claim_conversion_deliveries` are SECURITY DEFINER, granted to `orvion_integration`, and take an explicit tenant argument. Nothing in the current architecture makes the future flow impossible; it needs one function, not a redesign.
- **One vocabulary question rides with it, and is NOT decided here:** whether an AI-authored message is `sender_type_code = 'system'` (available today, and adequate) or a new `ai_agent` value. Canon owns the catalog; the answer is not needed until the integration is built.
- **A second, concrete integration requirement, measured 2026-09-01 during the care/conversation slice and recorded HERE rather than as its own row, because it is this finding's own subject matter:** the two halves of the thread are **not** equally idempotent. `conversations` carries `conversations_unique_external_idx` — `unique (tenant_id, channel_code, external_conversation_id) where external_conversation_id is not null` — so a redelivered thread cannot duplicate. **`conversation_messages.external_message_id` has no unique index at all**, so a redelivered *message* would. No producer exists today (all three RPCs are `authenticated`-only, per this row), so nothing can currently create the duplicate and this is not a live defect — but the inbound door must ship the matching partial unique index in the same package that creates the writer, not afterwards. It was NOT added now: the correct key depends on whether the provider's id is unique per tenant or per channel, which is a property of the provider being integrated and is not knowable in advance.
- **Also measured and NOT acted on:** `sender_type_code` and `message_direction_code` are independently caller-chosen on **both** doors — `app.send_conversation_message` takes each as a parameter and validates neither against the other — so `('customer','outbound')` is storable. That is not a two-door gap and not a defect to fix by derivation; which combinations are legal is a property of the messaging model this finding defers, and inventing the rule now would be the business policy `AGENTS.md §6` forbids.

### FIN-6 — an invoice could be declared PAID by anyone who could see it (discovered + FIXED 2026-08-29, `202607057100`)
- **Category:** financial integrity · **Severity:** High · **Status:** FIXED · **Owner:** engineering
- **Found while resolving SEC-2** — it is the half of SEC-2 that was never a business decision.
- **Reproduced, with the control built in.** An `employee` holding `CREATE_INVOICE = f` and `RECORD_PAYMENT = f`, on an invoice they can see because it belongs to their own booking:
  ```
  mark a 50,000 EGP invoice 'paid' with no payment  ->  SUCCEEDED, status_after = paid
  change the invoice AMOUNT                          ->  REFUSED: permission denied: CREATE_INVOICE
  ```
  The second line is what makes the first conclusive: the guard was present and working and simply did not cover the status.
- **Root cause.** `app.guard_financial_capability` charges the capability on UPDATE "only when a MONETARY column changes" — FIN-3's own words, and correct reasoning for `refunds` and `quotation_items`, whose status IS governed by `app.enforce_status_transition`. For `invoices` it was not: `app.status_transitions` has **zero** rows for invoices and **canon 26 defines no Invoice State Machine at all**, so the status was governed by nothing.
- **Impact:** declaring a customer's invoice settled without any money arriving. `reporting.customer_outstanding` and every balance derived from invoice status then report a debt as paid.
- **Remediation (applied):** `status_code` and `external_submission_status_code` join the guarded column list for `invoices`, and `external_submission_status_code` for `receipts` — the tax-submission state, the same class one column over (§26), where falsely claiming acceptance by the tax authority is the same kind of misstatement. Each charges the permission the table ALREADY charges; nothing new was decided. **Checked before writing:** the only writers of invoice status are `app.issue_invoice` and `app.record_payment`, and CREATE_INVOICE and RECORD_PAYMENT are held by exactly the same three roles (ceo, finance_manager, owner), so no working path breaks — proven by assertion 8, not assumed.
- **Deliberately NOT changed:** `refunds.refund_status_code` and the quotation status, both already governed by canon-defined machines; adding them would charge twice and imply the transition table is insufficient. And the ABSENCE of an Invoice State Machine — see FIN-7.
- **Test requirement (met):** `68_financial_status_capability_test.sql` (11) — the employee proven able to SEE the invoice first; every refusal asserted ON THE ROW as well as by exception; FIN-3's amount guard proven intact; **finance proven still able to advance the invoice**, which is the assertion that matters most; and SEC-2's intentional half pinned by a non-governed column on the same row still updating.

### FIN-6b — the financial guard named a column that does not exist, and so guarded nothing (discovered + FIXED 2026-08-29, `202607057100`)
- **Category:** guard integrity · **Severity:** Medium · **Status:** FIXED · **Owner:** engineering
- **Evidence:** `app.guard_financial_capability` mapped `receipts` to `array['amount']`. **`public.receipts` has no `amount` column** — the money lives on the payment it receipts. `to_jsonb(new) ->> 'amount'` is NULL on both sides, `NULL is distinct from NULL` is false, so `v_changed` never became true: **the UPDATE branch of that guard has been inert for receipts since FIN-3 shipped.**
- **Root cause, and it is a cost of the fix for an earlier bug.** The `to_jsonb` comparison exists because SPEC-159-A proved that naming `new.<column>` directly in a multi-table trigger makes plpgsql bind every referenced field before the CASE picks a branch. Comparing by name fixed that — and simultaneously removed the compiler's ability to notice a column that is not there. A wrong name now fails silently instead of loudly.
- **Remediation (applied):** `receipts` maps to `external_submission_status_code`, the column it actually has and the one worth guarding. Plus assertion 11, which checks **every** column in the map against `information_schema` — the assertion that would have caught this on the day it shipped.

### FIN-7 — canon defines no Invoice State Machine (recorded 2026-08-29)
- **Category:** canon completeness · **Severity:** Low–Medium · **Status:** BLOCKED — BUSINESS DECISION · **Owner:** owner
- **Evidence:** canon 26 defines SIXTEEN state machines — lead, booking, booking item, finance approval, document lifecycle, subscription, offline conversion delivery, trusted device, OTP, task, complaint, service request, quotation, conversation, marketing campaign — and **no invoice machine**. Yet `invoice_status_code` is a live catalog with six values (draft, issued, partially_paid, paid, voided, overdue) and `app.status_transitions` has no rows for invoices.
- **What FIN-6 did and did not fix:** FIN-6 governs **WHO** may change an invoice's status. It does not govern **WHICH** changes are legal — finance can still move `draft -> paid` directly, or back from `paid` to `draft`. Inventing the legal transitions would be inventing business policy, which is why it was not done.
- **Decision required (minimum to unblock):** the legal transitions between the six existing statuses, and whether `voided` is reachable at all (see VOID-1, which asks the same question from the columns' side). Everything else is already in place: the catalog, the guard, and the transition machinery that ten other tables already use.

### DOC-LC-1 — canon's Document Lifecycle machine was never wired to the runtime (recorded 2026-08-29)
- **Category:** lifecycle enforcement · **Severity:** Medium · **Status:** OPEN (derivable — next package) · **Owner:** engineering
- **Evidence:** canon 26 § Document Lifecycle State Machine defines three transitions — `active -> archived`, `active -> superseded`, `superseded -> archived` — and explicitly does NOT permit returning to `active`. `app.status_transitions` has **zero** rows for `documents`, and `app.enforce_status_transition` is not attached to it. `app.enforce_archive_authority` fires only when `is_archived` changes, so setting `lifecycle_status_code` directly walks past it.
- **Why it is OPEN rather than BLOCKED:** unlike FIN-7, canon HAS the machine. The permissions are derivable from the existing writers — ARCHIVE_DOCUMENT for the two `-> archived` transitions (what `app.archive_document` charges) and CREATE_DOCUMENT_VERSION for `-> superseded` (what `app.add_document_version` charges). Nothing needs deciding; it needs wiring plus behavioural proof that `archive_document` and `add_document_version` still work, which is why it is its own package rather than a line in this one.

### PAR-1b — I twice read "the repository's text" out of a database that was not the repository (discovered + FIXED 2026-08-29)
- **Category:** guard integrity / method · **Severity:** Medium · **Status:** FIXED · **Owner:** engineering
- **The correction, stated plainly.** The 2026-08-29 API-contract report said Primary's `app.document_retention_days` had been "restored to the repository's exact text". It had not. I read the text out of the LOCAL database at a moment when local had been hand-modified during the session, and pushed THAT onto Primary — so the fix moved Primary *away* from the repository while reporting the opposite.
- **How it surfaced:** deploying `202607057100` produced a PRIMARY FUNCTION DRIFT alarm on a function this package never touched. Narrowing found `document_retention_days` with local and Primary **swapped** relative to the previous session.
- **Settled by experiment rather than argument:** applying the migration file's own statement (`sed -n '104,111p'`) produced the 27-character form; a clean `npx supabase db reset` produced the same; the 20-character form existed only in a hand-modified local. **The repository form is the 27-character one, both environments now hold it, and `app.document_retention_days()` returns NULL on both — RET-1's retain-forever default was never at risk on either environment at any point.**
- **Root cause:** `supabase db reset` is the ONLY thing that makes local equal the repository. Applying a migration to local by hand mid-session — normal while iterating — breaks that equality, and everything read out of local afterwards is not the repository's text.
- **Remediation (applied):** `check_database_parity.ps1`'s header now states this and instructs that a drift report be answered by **resetting local and re-reading both sides before changing anything on Primary**. The source of truth is `supabase/migrations`, not any running database.

### GOV-1 — the boot chain's own pointer was five reports stale, and no guard checked it (discovered + FIXED 2026-08-29)
- **Category:** governance integrity · **Severity:** Medium · **Status:** FIXED · **Owner:** engineering
- **Evidence:** `reports/README.md` states its own rule — *"Whoever writes the next session report updates this row in the same commit — an unlinked report is invisible to the boot sequence, which is the one job this pointer has."* On 2026-08-29 the pointer still named `sla-1-the-escalation-that-never-fired-2026-08-29.md` while **five** later reports had been written: acquisition-lineage, scheduled-execution, care-journeys, api-contract and sec2/FIN-6. `AGENTS.md §4` Stage A step 7 requires every session to read that index before proposing work, so a fresh agent would have booted on a five-package-old view of the system.
- **Why no guard caught it:** `check_repository_consistency.ps1` Check 1 verifies that references RESOLVE — a different question from whether they are CURRENT. A pointer to a real file that is simply the wrong file passes every existing check.
- **Considered and rejected:** "every file in `reports/history/` must be referenced by the index". There are 77 history reports and the older ones were never individually indexed, so that rule would fail on ~60 historical files and be noise rather than signal. The convention is narrower than that, and the guard should encode the convention that exists rather than a stricter one nobody follows.
- **Remediation (applied):** the pointer is refreshed and chains all five skipped reports rather than dropping them, and **Check 10** compares it against the manifest's `Narrative:` field. Both are maintained every package by construction, so disagreement means one was forgotten. Proven in both directions before shipping: pointing the README at the old file produced `STALE POINTER`, restoring it cleared.

### SCHED-1 (INVESTIGATED + PARTIALLY ADDRESSED 2026-08-28, `202607056300`) — nothing invokes the storage executor
- **Category:** operations / scheduling · **Severity:** Medium · **Status:** BLOCKED — EXTERNAL DEPENDENCY (one owner-placed secret) + ARCHITECTURAL DECISION (which route) · **Owner:** owner
- **What actually needs scheduling, measured live:** three of the four recurring jobs already have pg_cron entries on Primary — `app.reconcile_document_storage` (daily 00:30), `app.process_lead_sla` (every minute), `app.process_subscription_lifecycle` (daily 00:10). The **storage executor** is the fourth and has no scheduler at all. It is an Edge Function (ACTIVE, `verify_jwt = true`), proven end to end by `scripts/verify_storage_end_to_end.ps1` — except that nothing ever calls it.
- **The three routes, costed on live evidence rather than preference:**
  - **A. pg_cron + pg_net + Vault.** `pg_cron` 1.6.4 is installed, `pg_net` 0.20.4 is available but NOT installed, and **`supabase_vault` IS installed** — which materially improves the position recorded when pg_net was first declined, because the invocation secret could live encrypted in Vault instead of in a migration, a column or a log. The cost is still real: installing pg_net gives the DATABASE outbound HTTP, a capability ORVION does not have today.
  - **B. n8n schedule → HTTP request.** n8n is live (0 workflows, 2 credentials, re-proven 2026-08-28) and keeps outbound HTTP out of the database, at the cost of making a core retention path depend on n8n's uptime.
  - **C. A scheduled GitHub Action**, which couples data-plane operations to CI.
- **Why it is blocked either way:** all three need the owner to place ONE secret, which never passes through the agent (AGENTS.md §6). Choosing between them is a security trade-off, not an engineering preference — installing pg_net unilaterally to make a metric move would be exactly the "select a scheduler because it is convenient" the directive forbids.
- **What needed no decision, and is done:** the gap was **silent**. If the executor never runs — or is scheduled and then breaks — retention-expired findings accumulate, bytes ORVION undertook to destroy stay on disk, and nothing says so. `app.storage_action_backlog()` (and its `public` wrapper, service_role only) reports pending actions, **the age of the oldest**, how many have already failed an attempt, the last attempt time, and the wider unresolved-finding count. It calls `app.claim_storage_actions` rather than restating its rules, because a monitor that measures a different population than the worker consumes reports zero while work piles up.
- **Test requirement (met):** `60_storage_backlog_observability_test.sql` (11) — zero under the default retain-forever policy (the monitor does not manufacture work from a null policy), then **the monitor's count asserted EQUAL to what the executor would claim**, the age proven to move, FND-1's failed-attempt contract proven visible, the RET-2 suspension exclusion proven to apply to both together, and the endpoint proven unreachable by anon and by an authenticated tenant user. Plus 4 HTTP assertions in `verify_storage_end_to_end.ps1`, including the same monitor-equals-worker check over the wire.
- **Decision required:** which route, and the one secret it needs.

### TRANS-2 — eight lead transitions were enforced for sequence and not for authority (discovered + FIXED 2026-08-28, `202607056200`)
- **Category:** authorization / lifecycle · **Severity:** High · **Status:** FIXED · **Owner:** engineering
- **Evidence:** `app.status_transitions` is the direct-DML half of transition authority, read by `app.enforce_status_transition`, and its only authorization column is `permission_key` — applied `if v_permission is not null`. Eight `leads` rows carry NULL: `assigned->contacted`, `contacted->qualified`, `qualified->quotation_sent`, `qualified->won`, `quotation_sent->negotiation`, `quotation_sent->won`, `negotiation->won`, `won->converted`.
- **Root cause:** the rule those transitions actually carry — *"the assigned handler, or ASSIGN_LEAD, plus MFA"*, stated verbatim by `app.advance_lead`, `app.convert_lead` and `app.record_lead_interaction` — has **no column that can express it**. A null therefore meant "no check at all" on the direct path while the RPCs refused anyone who was not the handler.
- **Impact:** direct DML could walk a COLLEAGUE'S lead from `contacted` to `won` and on to `converted` with no capability check. Reachable: `leads.scope_isolation` permits updating any lead the caller can see, and canon 28 gives `employee` `VIEW_DEPARTMENT_QUEUE` by ratified amendment — so an ordinary employee could move their whole department's pipeline. Marking a colleague's lead `won` is credit for a sale you did not make.
- **Remediation (applied):** `app.require_lead_handler(uuid)` holds the rule once, copied from the three RPCs that state it. `enforce_status_transition` calls it where `permission_key` is null on `leads`; `app.guard_lead_interaction_authority` calls it on `lead_interactions` (which closes SEC-1's last table by the same evidence). **Nothing became stricter than the RPC that walks it:** closure keeps `CLOSE_LEAD`, `assign_lead`'s `new->assigned` already carried `ASSIGN_LEAD`, and `process_lead_sla` is session-less and exempt.
- **The class, not the instance:** a null `permission_key` now means "apply this table's named fallback rule", and a table with neither a permission nor a fallback **FAILS CLOSED**. Only `leads` has nulls today, so the new branch is unreachable — which is the point: the next unguarded transition becomes a loud 42501 instead of a silent hole.
- **Test requirement (met):** `59_lead_handler_authority_test.sql` (14) — the colleague SEES the lead (positive control on the department queue), is then refused by direct DML *and* by the RPC, while the handler is permitted on the same lead and the same transition; ASSIGN_LEAD proven as the rule's other half; the closure path proven untouched; the system path proven still open; and "no table other than `leads` carries a null permission_key" pinned.

### TRANS-1 (INVESTIGATED 2026-08-28; NARROWED, and its guard repaired) — duplicated transition authority
- **Category:** architecture / maintainability · **Severity:** Medium · **Status:** BLOCKED — ARCHITECTURAL DECISION (narrowed) · **Owner:** owner
- **Correctness, measured rather than assumed:** all ten `advance_*` functions and all 104 `app.status_transitions` rows were compared exhaustively. **No live disagreement**: every transition the RPCs offer exists in the table, every permission both state matches, and the only table-side extras are three transitions performed by other RPCs (`assign_lead`, `record_lead_interaction`, `convert_lead`), which `advance_lead`'s own comment already documents.
- **The `event` column is NOT proven to be the right canonical home**, which the directive required be established before changing the schema. The inline lists carry more than an event: `advance_lead` carries `is_closure`, `advance_booking_item` carries sub-status handling, and the permission is per-transition in some functions and a single constant in others (`advance_refund` → `RECORD_REFUND`). Unifying the runtime source needs the table to carry per-entity extras — a wide sparse table or a jsonb payload — which is an architectural choice, not an obvious win. Deliberately not guessed.
- **Drift risk was real AND its guard was broken.** `54_transition_permission_parity_test` existed for exactly this and was checking **one function out of ten**: its regex matched only `('from','to','event','PERMISSION')`, which is `advance_booking`'s shape — `advance_quotation` puts the permission third, `advance_refund` has three elements, `advance_lead`'s fourth is a boolean. Its positive control ("at least 8 rules parsed") was satisfied by that one function alone. A guard that checks a tenth of what it claims is worse than none, because the next engineer trusts it.
